Leakage periods could later be used to evaluate whether forecast errors change under abnormal hydraulic conditions.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{0.9mm}\\r\\n\\\\scriptsize\\r\\n\\\\labeltext{Sources:} \\\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle} \\\\sepmark\\r\\n\\\\href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official Zenodo archive} \\\\sepmark\\r\\n\\\\href{https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001601}{Vrachimis et al. Like BattLeDIM, it also has strong support beyond Kaggle: the original challenge provides official datasets, attack-event information, the C-Town EPANET model and control materials, and the benchmark was documented in a peer-reviewed ASCE paper. C-Town is a medium-sized network with 388 junctions, 429 pipes, seven tanks, 11 pumps, five valves, nine programmable logic controllers, and a SCADA system. The released data are sampled hourly and contain 43 process variables: seven tank levels, 12 pressures, 12 flows (11 pumps plus one actuated valve), and 12 binary actuator states. The three principal files contain 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 mixed development records, and 2,089 test records, for 15,027 observations total.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.1mm}\\r\\n\\\\textbf{Proposed predictive-learning task.}\\r\\nI would use BATADAL for supervised regression: predict the recorded pressure head at a selected monitored location one hour ahead. Candidate predictors would include current and lagged tank levels, flows, actuator states, pressures, and calendar/time features, but not attack labels or any future actuator information. The hourly sampling interval makes this a different forecasting problem from BattLeDIM and provides a useful comparison of how temporal resolution affects prediction. I would select the target node after reviewing network topology and pressure behavior and compare an all-network predictor set with a smaller hydraulically related subset, such as variables from the same district or from pumps, valves, tanks, and boundary flows that influence the target area. A later error analysis could separate normal and attack periods; because some BATADAL attacks deliberately falsify SCADA readings, that analysis would concern the predictability of the recorded signals rather than automatically treating them as true physical pressure.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{0.9mm}\\r\\n\\\\scriptsize\\r\\n\\\\labeltext{Sources:} \\\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle} \\\\sepmark\\r\\n\\\\href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official BATADAL data} \\\\sepmark\\r\\n\\\\href{https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0000969}{Taormina et al. This provides a clear supervised-learning framework while avoiding leakage of future information into model training.\\r\\n\\\\end{contextbox}\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.6mm}\\r\\n\\\\begin{notebox}\\r\\n\\\\small\\r\\n\\\\labeltext{Feature scope and dimensionality.}\\r\\nThe full measurement sets are useful starting points, but I would not assume that every network variable belongs in the final model. After choosing the target sensor, the EPANET topology can define a hydraulically related subset---for example nearby pressures, inlet or boundary flows, connected tank levels, relevant pump or valve states, and local demand information where available. Comparing this physically motivated subset with an all-network baseline makes feature reduction a testable modeling decision rather than an arbitrary preprocessing step.\\r\\n\\\\end{notebox}\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n{\\\\color{Navy}\\\\bfseries\\\\large Why I am considering these two datasets}\\\\par\\r\\n\\\\vspace{0.5mm}\\r\\n\\\\small\\r\\nBoth benchmarks are directly related to pressure behavior in water-distribution systems, contain substantially more observations than candidate predictors, and include engineering context that can help interpret model inputs and errors. Participants in the BattLeDIM competition employ a range of methodologies to detect and localize leakage events, including engineering judgment, machine learning, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault diagnosis approaches .\nFigure 3: 82 AMR locations (nodes with red color) in “Area C” of L-Town. Nodes colored in blue do not have AMRs installed.\n\n3. COMPETITION GOAL\n\nThe L-Town utility has created an open call for teams to demonstrate their ability in detecting and localizing\nleakage events. The teams will be given a historical SCADA dataset along with information related with the\nleakages detected and fixed by the utility throughout 2018, to use for training purposes and for calibrating\ntheir models. It is possible that more leakage events occurred during 2018, however the utility was not able\nto detect and localize them.\n\nThroughout 2019, the utility conducted periodic surveys using additional sensing equipment, pipe inspections\nand other methods, and was able to detect and isolate all the leakage events that occurred within that period.\nThe most critical of these events were repaired, however it was not possible to repair some of these leakages\ndue to financial reasons.\n\nThe overall goal of this competition, is to identify methods which are able to detect and localize the leakage\nevents that occurred in L-Town in 2019, as fast as possible (with respect to time) and as accurately as possible\n(with respect to their location), in order to minimize their overall financial costs, both in water losses, as well\nas due to the hours spent in isolating the leakage by the utility staff. The L-Town utility will compare the dif-\nferent solutions and select the best one based on that objective. More details regarding the evaluation proce-\ndure will be released in January 2020.\n\n4. This is the single implementation roadmap and phase handoff.\n\n## Exact project requirements\n\nBuild a reproducible Python CSV research pipeline for water leakage detection. Implement all five hybrids below, study the relevant papers using their actual datasets and feature pipelines, reproduce their documented split, and additionally evaluate with mandatory 10-fold cross-validation. Compare results, identify a supported research gap, and test a specific improvement that addresses it. Keep code and evidence in the public GitHub repo.\n\nThe workflow is: load CSVs -> audit/preprocess -> check target, duplicate and suspicious feature leakage -> lock splits -> fit training-only preprocessing -> training-only SMOTE -> initial models -> diagnose overfitting/underfitting -> correct and retrain -> freeze settings -> final test and comparison.\n\nNo dashboard, deployment or full hydraulic-network application is required. M5 still requires a small, documented graph. Demand forecasting and abnormal consumption belong to the original problem; handle them as separate target-supported extensions. Do not invent an abnormal-consumption class from binary leak labels.\n\n## Required models and paper relationships\n\nID  | Our required hybrid  | Bounded implementation  | Related paper\n--- | --- | --- | ---\nM1  | 1D-CNN + LSTM  | Two Conv1D layers, unidirectional LSTM, classification head  | P5 is related; a closer reference remains to be selected\nM2  | CNN + BiLSTM + Attention  | CNN, historical-window BiLSTM, attention pooling, head  | P5 uses CNN/LSTM/attention; exact BiLSTM match unverified\nM3  | Autoencoder + LSTM  | Fold-trained encoder, latent sequence, LSTM classifier  | P2 studies AE, VAE and LSTM-AE; architecture differs\nM4  | Transformer + CNN  | Positional encoding, Transformer, temporal CNN, head  | P4 is a Transformer reconstruction reference\nM5  | GNN + LSTM  | Sensor graph convolution per time step, LSTM, head  | P3 uses graph reconstruction/prediction; architecture differs\n\nP1 is the BattLeDIM benchmark paper, not a CNN-LSTM architecture reference. The paper register holds titles, links, recipes and access limitations. These are candidate references, not five already reproducible experiments.\n\nAn 80/20 train-validation split is not automatically an 80/20 train-test split. Verify unknown details rather than guessing. Reproduce the paper's imbalance treatment; our SMOTE addition is a separate run if the paper does not use it.\n\n## Current evidence and limitations\n\nComponent  | Status\n--- | ---\nBattLeDIM download, sensor/event audit and boundary corrections  | Implemented; historical area labels fail classification feasibility\nLeakDB preparation  | Implemented: 175,200 rows, ten scenarios, 31 selected pressure channels\nLeakDB windowing and fold-local scaling/SMOTE  | Implemented; one real-fold smoke run completed\nVerification at previous phase boundary  | 33 tests passed and Ruff passed\nFive hybrids and common training runner  | Not implemented\nExact paper datasets/recipes  | Incomplete; see paper register\nPaper reproduction, trained CV and final accuracy tables  | Not completed\nResearch gap and novelty claim  | Not yet verified or selected\n\nThe prepared common configuration is configs/experiment.json. LeakDB source commit: `131144ba423a82639f881adab0d493ab50e3b2fb`; archive `CCWI-WDSA2018/Benchmarks/Hanoi_CMH.zip`, MD5 `700e10f8a90f028f838fcae49660225c`. Features: pressure Node 2 through Node 32, ordered numerically; Node 1 is constant zero. Target: leak presence at the last input timestamp. Window: 24 half-hour observations (12-hour coverage), stride 6 (3 hours). There are 21,810 development and 7,360 test windows.\n\nThe common benchmark uses Jan-Sep 2017 development and Oct-Dec locked test, with ten leave-one-scenario-out folds inside development. This grouped CV is distinct from expanding chronological CV. All training folds have both classes; six assessment scenarios have no positive development labels. Report aggregate out-of-fold scores and undefined per-fold metrics explicitly. Counts alone do not prove scenario independence: audit shared simulation sources and duplicate windows before training. Three-hour sampling limits detection-delay resolution; freeze a denser protocol before experiments if rapid alerts are the gap being studied. Test windows currently allow observed past context; report this operational protocol and boundary-carryover events explicitly.\n\nThe feasibility report records the dataset decision and smoke counts. Existing `configs/targets.json` and `configs/splits.json` describe the BattLeDIM audit, not the accepted five-model classification protocol.\n\n## Existing commands (preparation only)\n\nPowerShell, Python 3.11+:\n    \n    python -m venv .venv\n    .\\.venv\\Scripts\\python.exe -m pip install -e \".[dev,ml]\"\n    .\\.venv\\Scripts\\python.exe -m ruff check .\n    .\\.venv\\Scripts\\python.exe -m pytest -q\n\nIf the ignored LeakDB source folder is absent, acquire the pinned source once:\n    \n    git clone https://github.com/KIOS-Research/LeakDB.git data/raw/LeakDB-source\n    git -C data/raw/LeakDB-source checkout --detach 131144ba423a82639f881adab0d493ab50e3b2fb\n    .\\.venv\\Scripts\\smart-water.exe prepare-leakdb\n    .\\.venv\\Scripts\\smart-water.exe preprocess-leakdb\n\n`preprocess-leakdb` runs one SMOTE fold smoke check, not ten-fold model training. Its defaults match the current common configuration; it does not yet load paper configs. Inspect `smart-water --help` for retained BattLeDIM download, prepare, audit, inventory, targets, splits, leakage-audit, cv-feasibility and seasonal-flow baseline commands. The seasonal baseline is a preparation sanity check, not a trained leak classifier. Neural dependencies are available through `.[deep]` when Phase 5 starts.\n\n## Repository and phase handoff rules\n\n  * `README.md`: sole requirements, execution steps and current status.\n  * `docs/PAPERS_AND_REPRODUCTION.md`: cited papers and recipe/access evidence.\n  * `docs/datasets.md`: dataset provenance and alternatives.\n  * `docs/results/`: compact observed audit and research results; historical findings are labeled. `docs/verification.md` records the original starter environment/results.\n  * `src/smart_water/`, `tests/`, `configs/`: reusable implementation, checks and contracts.\n  * `data/`, `reports/`, `models/`: local ignored data and large outputs.       "title": "combines six algorithms, including changepoint detection, non-parametric multiple changepoint analysis, divisive hierarchical estimation, kernel changepoint analysis, and Bayesian estimation of abrupt changes and trends. Huang et al. [11] propose a method consisting of five stages: model decomposition, data partitioning, nodal demand calibration, calibration residual-based leakage detection, and an improved vectorial angle method for leakage localization. The Leakbusters team, represented by Daniel et al. [12], developed a method with two algorithm components. The first component analyzes pressure differences between pairs of nodes using SCADA data to identify leakage events. The model is trained on normal time periods, and the reconstruction error is used to detect leakage events. Saldarriaga et al. [13] utilize a genetic algorithm (GA) to identify leaks, while Wang et al. [14] from the Tsinghua Team employ a multistage approach involving empirical mode decomposition, extraction of daily and weekly seasonalities, and emitter representation. The Under Pressure team, represented by Steffelbauer et al. [15], introduces a hierarchical approach for leak diagnosis. It involves demand calibration using AMR data and measured flows, mathematical optimization for calibrating pipe roughness, and the use of a dual network for leak start time detection and localization. Zhang et al. [16] employ a fuzzy similarity priority ratio for leakage localization, while Romero et al. [17] from the IRI team combine a model-based and data-driven methodology. Their approach determines the usage of the two approaches based on the characteristics of the network’s different areas. Min et al. [18] and Blocher et al. [19] both use clustering algorithms to solve the task, and Liu et al. [20] employ an LSTM. Dopazo et al. [21] and Tan et al. [22] utilize machine learning approaches.",

Github: https://github.com/SWN-group-at-TU-Berlin/LILA\n\n  * Image: LILA Logo© FG Smart Water Networks\n\n## LILA - Leakage Identification and Localization Algorithm\n\nSeveral anomalies and infrastructure failures cause inefficiencies in urban water systems. In this context, leakages in water distribution networks are the biggest cause of water loss and estimated to globally cost 39 billion USD per year. Data-driven tools can support the prevention and mitigation of such failures.\nAs part of this line of research line we have developed LILA, a high-resolution pressure-driven algorithm for leakage detection in water distribution networks. A first version of the algorithm was awarded the third place at the international Battle of Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM 2020), and a journal article has been recently published in the Journal of Water Resources Planning and Management.\n\nArticle DOI: https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001535\n\nGithub: https://github.com/SWN-group-at-TU-Berlin/LILA\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nABSTRACT\nPESANTEZ SARMIENTO, JORGE E. Using Smart (https://repository.lib.ncsu.edu/bitstreams/f0f1a26d-ff26-4fba-93f5-74e598304092/download)\nciteturn2search13 [wordlim: 200] Published: 6 months ago; please refer to Vrachimis and Eliades (2020) and this public repository: https://github. ... LILA and compare it with the BattLeDIM benchmark.\nFinally, we compute the aggregate economic score S deﬁned by the BattLeDIM organizers\nto account for the trade-oﬀbetween the costs a water utility has to bear to send out a repair\ncrew when an alarm is raised and the beneﬁts (i.e., NRW reduction) the utility can obtain by\naccurately identifying and localizing leakages (for more details and code for implementation,\nplease refer to Vrachimis and Eliades (2020) and this public repository: https://github.\ncom/KIOS-Research/BattLeDIM).\n3.5\nResults\nIn this section, we present the numerical results obtained for leakage identiﬁcation and\nlocalization by LILA on the L-Town dataset introduced in the previous section. We compare\nthe results obtained by LILA with the benchmark results we obtained with an earlier\nversion of the algorithm (hereafter called BattLeDIM benchmark), which we submitted for\ncomparative assessment to the BattLeDIM competition (Daniel et al., 2020). Update 8/9/2020: A participating team, IRI, used the online code to reproduce their results, and realized that they had sent us their solutions in the wrong date format.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\ncombines six algorithms, including changepoint detection, non-parametric multiple changepoint analysis, divisive hierarchical estimation, kernel changepoint analysis, and Bayesian estimation of abrupt changes and trends. Huang et al. [11] propose a method consisting of five stages: model decomposition, data partitioning, nodal demand calibration, calibration residual-based leakage detection, and an improved vectorial angle method for leakage localization. The Leakbusters team, represented by Daniel et al. [12], developed a method with two algorithm components. The first component analyzes pressure differences between pairs of nodes using SCADA data to identify leakage events. The model is trained on normal time periods, and the reconstruction error is used to detect leakage events. Saldarriaga et al. [13] utilize a genetic algorithm (GA) to identify leaks, while Wang et al. [14] from the Tsinghua Team employ a multistage approach involving empirical mode decomposition, extraction of daily and weekly seasonalities, and emitter representation. The Under Pressure team, represented by Steffelbauer et al. [15], introduces a hierarchical approach for leak diagnosis. It involves demand calibration using AMR data and measured flows, mathematical optimization for calibrating pipe roughness, and the use of a dual network for leak start time detection and localization. Zhang et al. [16] employ a fuzzy similarity priority ratio for leakage localization, while Romero et al. [17] from the IRI team combine a model-based and data-driven methodology. Their approach determines the usage of the two approaches based on the characteristics of the network’s different areas. Min et al. [18] and Blocher et al. [19] both use clustering algorithms to solve the task, and Liu et al. [20] employ an LSTM. Dopazo et al. [21] and Tan et al. [22] utilize machine learning approaches. (https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-3630/LWDA2023-paper40.pdf)\nciteturn2search15 [wordlim: 200] Published: 2.7 years ago; The Leakbusters team, represented by Daniel et al. The below District Metered Area is that of Álftanes in the capital region of Iceland. The import procedure of the GIS model and its conversion to a graph is contained in the directory\n\n# `02 Data Pre-Processing`\n\nThis directory holds notebooks where pre-processing and Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA) is performed on the time-series sensor data accommodating the real-world data from Álftanes DMA.\n\n# `03 Hydraulic Simulations`\n\nVarious open-source datasets are available for the leakage detection problem. The approach for this project will be to develop a graph-based learning method for leakage detection on such a dataset, validating it on an open-source benchmark for comparison with the state-of-the-art, and finally applying the learned model, or the methodology, to the real-world data in `01-02`.\n\nThis folder thus contains `Python` scripts and `Jupyter` development notebooks for working with the BattLeDIM (Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods) dataset.\n\nThis dataset delivers the hypothetical WDN of L-Town, the topology of which is contained within an EPANET input file, `.inp`. EPANET is a hydraulic simulation tool, and a niche toolset is required to import the model and convert it to a `networkx` graph. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nGitHub - SUBHRO71/Smart-Water-Distribution-and-Leakage-Detection: Python research starter for water demand forecasting and leakage detection using BattLeDIM, with dataset comparisons and a CNN-LSTM modeling plan. · GitHub (https://github.com/SUBHRO71/Smart-Water-Distribution-and-Leakage-Detection)\nciteturn3search1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 3 weeks ago; Python research starter for water demand forecasting and leakage detection using BattLeDIM, with dataset comparisons and a CNN-LSTM modeling plan.\n## About\n\nPython research starter for water demand forecasting and leakage detection using BattLeDIM, with dataset comparisons and a CNN-LSTM modeling plan.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nLeak Detection in Water Distribution Networks (https://www.eurasip.org/Proceedings/Eusipco/Eusipco2025/pdfs/0002407.pdf)\nciteturn3search12 [wordlim: 200] Published: 1.2 years ago; Graph neural network-based method for leakage risk assessment in water ... The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch.\n## 3. Experimental setups\n\n### 3.1. Dataset\n\nReal-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.\n\nImage: Fig 2\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 2. Nodal features of l-Town Network. (a) Nodal height features (b) Nodal type features (c) Variation of different water usage types over a year.\n\nThe l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the model’s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.\n\nThe pressurized pumping station that provides additional pressure to the high zone is represented as a node with known pressures for prediction. Its nodal pressures were obtained from pump characteristic curves and flow sensors installed on the pump. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nReview on Information Fusion‐Based Data Mining for Improving Complex Anomaly Detection - Moldovan - 2025 - WIREs Data Mining and Knowledge Discovery - Wiley Online Library (https://wires.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/widm.70017)\nciteturn3search3 [wordlim: 200] Published: 1.4 years ago; Crawled: 3 weeks ago; GitHub. https://github.com/FangYukun1996/Deep-Shrinkage-Autoencoder-for-Sensor-Anomaly-Detection/tree/9a6adf225401d47276266aeb51fd7d4b946111be. ... In the first step, the nominal hydraulic model without leaks is simulated using the Water Network Tool for Resilience (WNTR) Python toolkit (Klise et al. 2017), and the pressures at all junctions with sensors is stored for the years 2018 and 2019. The pressure measurements are then split into train-validation datasets in an 80–20 ratio. The training and validation data are z-standardised for zero mean and unit variance to enable efficient model training. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nCN119129798A - 一种城市供水管网节点压力的预测方法 - Google Patents (https://patents.google.com/patent/CN119129798A/zh)\nciteturn3search6 [wordlim: 200] Published: 1.8 years ago; Crawled: 3 months ago; Forecasting(X^{*})＝f_{Θ}(X^{*})＝Z。为了更好的说明本实例提供方法的技术效果，以使用真实公开示例管网L Town的创建需水压力预测过程为例，进一步阐述本公开提供的供水管网未知节点压力估计方法的实现过程。\n对训练好的模型输入非传感器节点的时空嵌入向量即可得到缺少压力传感器的预测结果，即预测过程。具体过程的包含：\n\n给定I个布置有传感器的节点压力数据，取其历史J个时间步长的观测值作为训练数训练的任务是预测未来J^{*}个时间步的压力值目标函数为：\n\n其中，Θ代表模型所有可训练参数。\n\n将目标用水节点的位置信息和预测时间点输入至节点压力预测模型，以获得目标用水节点的节点压力数据，更具体地，在获得I个布置有传感器节点的训练模型之后，压力预测通过输入全部I+I^{*}个节点 得到：\n\nForecasting(X^{*})＝f_{Θ}(X^{*})＝Z。\n\n为了更好的说明本实例提供方法的技术效果，以使用真实公开示例管网L Town的创建需水压力预测过程为例，进一步阐述本公开提供的供水管网未知节点压力估计方法的实现过程。在本实施例中所用的示例管网共有782个用用水节点、2个供水点、1个减压阀、1台加压泵，节点间最大高程差为71.21m。--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nDetection and localization of pipeline leakage of water distribution networks based on graph convolutional networks - Li - 2025 - Computer-Aided Civil and Infrastructure Engineering - Wiley Online Library (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/mice.70173)\nciteturn3search7 [wordlim: 200] Published: 10 months ago; Crawled: yesterday; Daniel et al. Accordingly, numerous studies have evaluated leakage-detection algorithms on this benchmark. Daniel et al. (2022) employed a two-stage framework combining sequential detection and model-based localization; a semi-supervised 1D-CNN autoencoder was used to reconstruct pressure signals for leak localization(Tornyeviadzi & Seidu, 2023); a graph-based state interpolation (GSI) was performed to complete hydraulic states and applied dictionary-learning (DL) classification, achieving localization from pressure series sampled at 5-min intervals(Irofti et al., 2022); Ma et al. (2022) combined SCADA-driven state estimation with real-time demand calibration and history–simulation differencing for leakage detection; Wan et al. Therefore, incorporating historical pressure trends and adaptive temporal models could enhance prediction accuracy and transferability.\n\nAs illustrated in Fig. 3a, the pressure density distributions for datasets (A) and (B) in C-Town indicate that modifying the pipe attributes led to 1.7 % of pressure values clustering at 50 m, whereas networks with only hydraulic condition adjustments exhibited a smoother distribution, with a maximum pressure of approximately 110 m and a density of just 0.8 %. Additionally, to simulate limited sensor coverage, 30 % of nodes were selected as sensor locations using the method described in Section 3.1, and pressure data were collected accordingly. As shown in Fig. 3b, the boxplots of C-Town, L-Town, and Ky13 remain largely consistent, demonstrating that the selected sensor nodes effectively capture the overall pressure distribution within each network. Notably, outliers are observed in L-Town and Ky13 due to long-distance pipelines in their topology, which lead to large pressure differences between peripheral and central nodes, posing challenges for transfer learning.\n\nImage: Fig 3\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (261KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 3. Pressure distribution characteristics of WDNs under different configurations: (a) Probability density of pressure values for datasets with different pipe attributes and hydraulic conditions in C-Town; (b) Boxplots comparing the overall pressure distribution and the sensor-covered pressure distribution in C-Town, L-Town, and Ky13.\n\nNotably, for the same topology, compared with “L-Town to C-Town” transfer, all four metrics show significant improvement, with MAE reduced from 36.55 m to 22.47 m and MAPE dropping from 34.94 % to 26.26 %. This suggests that within the same topology, structural similarities facilitate transfer learning, whereas topological differences hinder effective knowledge transfer.\n\nOur proposed transfer learning approach with three Heter-GATRes blocks and achieves the lowest MAE of 7.31 m (C-Town to C-Town and L-Town to C-Town) and MAPE of 9.15 % (C-Town to C-Town) and 9.43 % (L-Town to C-Town), outperforming all baseline methods. Under noisy conditions, GASTL still achieves an R² of 0.88, reaching a level suitable for practical application. Increasing the number of Hetero-GATRes blocks (GASTL 10 blocks) does not yield further improvements, indicating the risk of overfitting issues in Source WDNs. Additionally, the No Shift model produces results comparable to GASTL (3 blocks), but in the same topology, R² (0.91) and RMSE (10.25 m) of No Shift perform better, whereas in L-Town to C-Town, MAE (7.23 m) and MAPE (9.32 %) are higher. This suggests that in the same topology, shift parameters make cross-domain alignment more challenging, while in different topologies, they provide better adaptation.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nUvA-DARE (Digital Academic Repository) (https://pure.uva.nl/ws/files/234144034/engproc-69-00050.pdf)\nciteturn3search13 [wordlim: 200] Published: 4 months ago; Data-Driven Deep Learning Approaches for Water ... A particularly powerful technique is the wavelet scattering transform (WST), which creates a stable signal representation and is invariant with translation, enabling the extraction of robust features without needing manual feature engineering or even complex deep learning models (Martinez-Rios et al., 2024).\nL-TOWN benchmark  | Simulation/real-world comparison (pressure data)  | (Rajabi et al., 2023)  | Methods are limited by the requirement for accurately calibrated hydraulic models and exact measurements of demand\n\nOpen in a new tab\n\n#### 3.2.2. Advanced signal processing and feature extraction\n\nRaw sensor data, especially high-frequency acoustic and vibration signals, need to be transformed to extract discriminative features that a machine learning model can effectively utilize (Martinez-Rios et al., 2024; Saravanabalaji et al., 2023). The different ways of performing this can be categorized as time frequency transformation, feature engineering, and dimensionality reduction.\n\n##### 3.2.2.1. Time-frequency transformation\n\nDue to leak signals often being non-stationary, methods that utilize and analyze data in both time and frequency domains can be beneficial. Examples of this can be seen in the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT), a technique used in literature (Saravanabalaji et al., 2023) that uses and computes Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT) to convert the collected acoustic signals from their original time domain to the frequency domain, whereas (Liu et al., 2024), carried out a Short Time Fourier Transform (STFT) to create spectrograms of collected acoustic signals and analyze the difference between signals from leaking pipes and those from normal, non-leaking pipes. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nJoint anomaly detection and topology inference for dynamic water distribution networks monitoring - ScienceDirect (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.iot.2026.101978)\nciteturn3search11 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3 months ago; Crawled: last month; Leveraging learned or known demand profiles could improve robustness to demand variability and enhance the detection of weaker or short-lived anomalies. ... Experiments on realistic WDNs (Branched and L-Town), further confirmed the qualitative capability of the proposed approach to infer higher-order cells localized around leaking pipes and to produce anomaly scores that highlight persistent leak signatures even in the presence of transient demand fluctuations. ... Third, we will incorporate stronger hydraulic priors into the learning process, such as flow conservation constraints and pressure-flow relations, with the goal of improving physical consistency.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nPowerPoint Presentation (https://www.calstate.edu/impact-of-the-csu/research/water/Documents/conference/2023/19-using-smart-meters-to-improve-the-management-of-water-distribution-system.pdf)\nciteturn3search15 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3.5 years ago; - **Pressure uniformity across the network** ... ### Forecasting Demand with Advanced Metering Infrastructure (AMI) ... Repository files navigation\nL198: \nL199:   *   * cite86†README L200:   * cite86†GPL-3.0 license L201: \nL202: More items\nL203: # LILA - the sequential pressure-based algorithm for data-driven Leakage Identification and model-based Localization\nL204: This repository contains the Leakage Identification part of LILA - the sequential pressure-based algorithm for data-driven Leakage Identification and model-based Localization in water distribution networks. LILA identifies potential leakages via semisupervised linear regression of pairwise sensor pressure data and provides the location of theis nearest sensor.\nL205: LILA also locates leaky pipes relying on an initial set of candidate pipes and a simulation-based optimization framework with iterative linear and mixed-integer linear programming.\nL206: ### Citation\nL207: If you use LILA or part of its code, please consider citing cite87†our paper†ascelibrary.org that describes it:\nL208: \nL209:     `Daniel, I., Pesantez, J., Letzgus, S., Khaksar Fasaee, M.A., Alghamdi, F., Berglund, E., Mahinthakumar, G.\nL210:     and Cominola, A., 2022. A Sequential Pressure-Based Algorithm for Data-Driven Leakage Identification and\nL211:     Model-Based Localization in Water Distribution Networks. Journal of Water Resources Planning and Management,\nL212:     148(6), p.04022025. DOI:10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001535\nL213:     `\nL214: ### Authors\nL215: \nL216:   * cite88†Ivo Daniel†www.swn.tu-berlin.de , cite89†Andrea Cominola†www.swn.tu-berlin.de - cite90†Chair of Smart Water Networks†swn.tu-berlin.de | cite91†Technische Universität Berlin†tu.berlin and cite92†Einstein Center Digital Future, Berlin†digital-future.berlin (Germany)\nL217:   * Simon Letzgus - cite93†Machine Learning group†www.ml.tu-berlin.de | cite91†Technische Universität Berlin†tu.berlin (Germany)\nL218: The above authors contributed to the content of this repository. For the full team of authors of LILA, refer to the authors of the above-cited paper.\nL219: ### Awards\nL220: \nL221: An early version of LILA was awarded the Third Place Award for outstanding achievement in the cite94†BattLeDIM 2020†battledim.ucy.ac.cy , the Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods. Check out this presentation by our Leakbusers team here: cite95†https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PrHgNnQn-lQ†www.youtube.com L222: \nL223: ## Getting started with LILA\nL224: \nL225: LILA operates in a sequential way, represented in this flowchart: source: underlying work (Daniel et al., 2022)\nL226: ### Leakage Identification module\nL227: The cite96†`2019_leakage_identification.ipynb` notebook in folder cite97†LI performs the leakage identification task, which is designed to raise an alarm about the presence of a potential leak. Firstly, the water distribution network and the time series of pressure at network nodes provided as input are analyzed as a whole to visualize all occurring leakages simultaneously. Thereafter, each identified leak is investigated individually to estimate a precise leak start time.\nL228: In general, the leakage identification module performs two sequential steps:\nL229:   1. Linear regression analysis is performed on pairs of time series from pressure data and the residual error of the model is calculated.\nL230:   2. Change point detection is applied to the residual from the pairwise linear regression analysis to provide the starting times of the leaks.\nL231: \nL232: The final section of the cite96†notebook also provides the code used to generate the following figures in Daniel et al. (2022):\nL233:   * Fig. 3: sensitivity analysis\nL234:   * Fig. 4: comparison of residual error vs. ground truth\nL235:   * Fig. 5: exemplary residual error trajectories during leakages with annotations\nL236:   * Fig. 6: result summary for leakage time-to-detection and total lost water volume\nL237:   * Fig. 7: result summary for leakage detection distance\nL238:   * Fig. 9: study of leak-to-noise ratio and cusum hyperparameters\nL239:   * Fig. 10: time series of residual error for individual leaks used for change point detection\nL240: ### Leakage Localization module\nL241: \nL242: Part of the code to run the Leakage localization module of LILA is availablle in this repository: cite98†https://github.com/jorgeps86/LeakLocalization L243: \nL244: ### Dependencies\nL245: \nL246: Dependencies and package version are listed in cite84†`dependencies.md` L247: ## Dataset\nL248: \nL249: The work in this repository is applied to the dataset of the cite94†BattLeDIM 2020†battledim.ucy.ac.cy , the Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods. The BattLeDIM dataset is open and available at the links below. Information on the BattLeDIM can be found at the following links:\nL250:   * Official website of the BattLeDIM, hosted by the BattLeDIM committee: cite94†https://battledim.ucy.ac.cy/†battledim.ucy.ac.cy L251:   * BattLeDIM problem description and rules: cite99†https://zenodo.org/record/3902046†zenodo.org L252:   * BattLeDIM dataset: cite100†https://zenodo.org/record/4017659#.X4mBaC2w1hE†zenodo.org L253:   * BattLeDIM results: cite101†https://zenodo.org/record/4139603#.X8lAfbG5p04.mendeley†zenodo.org L254: ## References\nL255: \nL256: LILA is fully presented and tested in Daniel et al. (2022):\nL257: \nL258:     `Daniel, I., Pesantez, J., Letzgus, S., Khaksar Fasaee, M.A., Alghamdi, F., Berglund, E., Mahinthakumar, G.\nL259:     and Cominola, A., 2022. A Sequential Pressure-Based Algorithm for Data-Driven Leakage Identification and\nL260:     Model-Based Localization in Water Distribution Networks. Journal of Water Resources Planning and Management,\nL261:     148(6), p.04022025. DOI:10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001535\nL262:     `\nL263: LILA derives from an initial version of the algorithm presented in cite102†Daniel et al. ### Message

```text
Encontré dos antecedentes útiles para comparar: LILA, que usa regresión lineal entre pares de sensores y detección de cambios, y modelos de grafos que reconstruyen presiones usando la topología de la red. Voy a guardar versiones identificables del código y revisar su preparación de datos y evaluación.

También conservaré las métricas con su contexto: el puntaje económico de BattLeDIM mide detección y localización de fugas, y no puede compararse directamente con MAE o RMSE de un pronóstico.
```

Mahinthakumar, M.ASCE7; and Andrea Cominola, A.M.ASCE8\nAbstract: Leakages in water distribution networks (WDNs) are estimated to globally cost 39 billion USD=year and cause water and revenue\nlosses, infrastructure degradation, and other cascading effects. Their impacts can be prevented and mitigated with prompt identification and\naccurate leak localization. In this work, we propose the leakage identification and localization algorithm (LILA), a pressure-based algorithm\nfor data-driven leakage identification and model-based localization in WDNs. First, LILA identifies potential leakages via semisupervised\nlinear regression of pairwise sensor pressure data and provides the location of their nearest sensors. Second, LILA locates leaky pipes relying\non an initial set of candidate pipes and a simulation-based optimization framework with iterative linear and mixed-integer linear program-\nming. LILA is tested on data from the L-Town network devised for the Battle of Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods. Results show that\nLILA can identify all leakages included in the data set and locate them within a maximum distance of 374 m from their real location. Abrupt\nleakages are identified immediately or within 2 h, while more time is required to raise alarms on incipient leakages. Specifically, we train two Chebyshev polynomial kernel Graph Convolutional Networks for the task of prediction, and reconstruction of nodal pressures in a WDN. Comparing the two network outputs (a predicted healthy model state with a reconstructed observation) a residual signal is obtained and analysed to detect leakages. By exploiting topological properties in the proposed approach, leakage isolation is also performed. We benchmark our method on the BattLeDIM 2020 dataset.\n\nCopyright © 2022 The Authors. This is an open access article under the CC BY-NC-ND license  \n(https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/)\n\nKeywords: Fault detection and diagnosis, water distribution systems, geometric deep learning.\n\n1. INTRODUCTION\n\nWater is commonly supplied to households through a Water Distribution Network (WDN). Kingdom et al. (2006) found that globally on average, around 35% of produced water is lost during distribution, with strong societal and economic implications. It is therefore fundamental to develop efficient techniques for the prompt detection and isolation of leakages in WDNs. However, major obstacles are represented by the scarcity of measurements and the uncertainty in demand, making the leakage isolation problem a very challenging one.\n\nMethods for detecting the presence of leaks in WDNs can be classified as passive and active. Passive leak detection systems are those that require maintenance personnel to survey a particular site in search of leaks (Chan et al. (2018)). Specifically, the proposed method detects leaks as transient oscillations in the vibration signals, using a convolutional neural network (CNN). After leak detection, a localisation algorithm is proposed using the graph-based wavelet method introduced in Srirangarajan et al. (2013), showing good performance. However, the latter has been carried out with engineered tests at night, resulting in unrealistic test conditions Wu and Liu (2017).
